## The object everyone must begin with

The source image is a rectangle of characters, not an approximate visual logo. There are nineteen rows, each containing thirty-eight ASCII bytes. Spaces, punctuation, lower-case q, the digit 1, and repeated z characters all matter. The eighty characters belonging to the four visible Base64 fields must be distinguished from the structural glyphs outside those fields.

![Canonical artwork with editorial field colours](figures/canonical_artwork.png)

*Figure 1. The exact nineteen-row source, with editorial colours identifying the four native fields and structural glyph classes. Colour is explanatory and is not part of the Bitcoin payload. Row and column numbers are zero-based. ([V01](#v01-editorial-verification))*

The separator-free byte sequence has SHA-256:

```text
869020c3e598955bfc71244923c1fb557a5e0d4cadd89443012c8232bfa618d7
```

The version with a line feed after every row has 741 bytes and a different SHA-256:

```text
96534d9faf3b6260987939fdb6994cd5b97eed18a175b0620c4ea5256c0c5607
```

Both are useful serializations. They must not be compared as though they were the same byte string. A screenshot, proportional font, trimmed text field, or changed newline convention can preserve a visual resemblance while changing the object being tested. ([V01](#v01-editorial-verification))

The preservation collection contains the row strings, the 722-byte flat form, and the line-delimited display form. The main narrative uses zero-based coordinates unless a source explicitly requires a different convention. A half-open interval such as columns [7:31) includes column 7 and stops before column 31.

## A short glossary before the technical chapters

| Term | Meaning in this investigation |
|---|---|
| ASCII byte | The numerical encoding of one printed character or space in the original artwork. |
| Base64 | A printable encoding used by the four visible fields. Decoding gives bytes; it does not decrypt them. |
| AES-GCM | The authenticated-encryption scheme used in the primary solve. A successful tag check validates the exact key, nonce, ciphertext, tag, and associated-data combination. |
| Nonce | An input to an encryption or signature operation. A nonce is not automatically a password or an instruction. |
| Authentication tag | The GCM value checked when decrypting. It is stronger evidence for that decryption than an attractive-looking plaintext alone. |
| BIP39 | The word-list and checksum system used by the public quest mnemonic and by many later experimental numerical readers. |
| U11 | An eleven-bit unsigned value from 0 to 2047. It can index the BIP39 English list; a pair of such indexes is not automatically a valid mnemonic. |
| ART | The BIP39 label at index 102, used as a retained arithmetic constant in several branches. |
| Q | The common public key recovered and verified for the Bitcoin construction. It is a mathematical authority label, not a person's identity. |
| r and s | The two scalar components of an ECDSA signature. Their representation and ordering matter in experimental readers. |
| DER | The structured encoding used for the construction's transaction ECDSA signatures. The 145 historical Bitcoin Signed Message signatures use a separate 65-byte compact encoding: header, r, and s. Byte offsets depend on the exact encoded object. |
| Witness | Transaction data carrying signatures and related spending information. A different valid signature can still be a different committed witness. |
| OP_RETURN | A Bitcoin script operation used here to publish data that cannot be spent through that output. |
| Hash | A digest of exact bytes. Equal text appearance does not guarantee equal byte hashes. |
| Commitment | A relationship binding an object to data, subject to the actual hash/signature/consensus rules that establish it. |
| GLOBAL40 | A retained forty-item numerical table derived under a declared field organization. Every lookup still requires an explicit index and representation. |
| CONTROL98 | The ninety-eight two-bit structural controls, giving a 192-bit body and four-bit trailing check under the retained reader. |
| LOW and FULL | Parity readers applied to the low nibble and full ASCII byte in the retained model. They are two views of a character's encoding, not two photographs. |
| Occupancy | Whether a cell is a space or contains a printed character. This is different from the ink shape within the cell. |
| Source owner | The identity of the original character that supplied a later displayed cell. Identical glyphs can have different owners. |
| Aperture or cavity | A connected region of blank cells through or from which a geometric reader is defined. |
| First hit | The first opaque cell or glyph encountered along a declared ray. Later characters can be occluded. |
| T30 | The specific half-turn T(r,c) = (30-r,37-c), used in the larger frame comparisons. |
| U | The separate local tile half-turn U(r,c) = (27-r,30-c). It must not be substituted for T30. |
| Projected or virtual cell | A position produced by an extension, reflection, or display construction. It is not an extra published Bitcoin character. |
| Renderer | A procedure turning inputs into a character image. Reproducing the final image does not alone identify its historical production process. |
| Holdout | Information withheld from a fitting procedure. A later mask over a previously inspected target is a useful isolation test, but not a historically blind discovery. |
| Counterexample | An explicit alternative satisfying a proposed rule while defeating a claimed conclusion. Its value is bounded by that rule's stated scope. |
| Consumer | The operation or object that is supposed to use a previously calculated result. A meaningful word without a selected consumer can remain an endpoint. |
| Supersession | A documented correction replacing an earlier interpretation while preserving the earlier evidence and calculation. |

## How the evidence labels should be read

| Label | What is established | What is not automatically established |
|---|---|---|
| Authenticated | A specified native check succeeds in its own domain. | Every surrounding interpretation, a human identity, or a second protocol's authority. |
| Direct observation | A feature is present in an identified source. | Its intended meaning. |
| Mechanical | A specified computation gives the stated result. | Why that computation should have been selected. |
| Supported interpretation | Evidence favours a reading with explicit alternatives. | Certainty or cryptographic authentication. |
| Conditional | The result follows if named assumptions are admitted. | Those assumptions themselves. |
| Rejected within scope | A defined test or family fails. | The failure of every possible encoding or route. |
| Open | A required source, operation, selection, or verification relationship is missing. | That the missing relationship cannot exist. |

The later chapters use these distinctions where they affect a conclusion. They are not a reason to stop documenting useful results. They make it possible to continue from the actual frontier instead of repeatedly rediscovering an old success or reviving a rejected assumption. (B:Evidence language)

## What was rechecked for this edition

The edition's offline verification reconstructs the canonical artwork and checks the AES-GCM solution, the structural counts, CONTROL98's four-bit check, the equal-code glyph-pair counts, the additional literal-choice reconstruction, the original blank components, the qzzq move, the eleven unmatched roof cells under T30, and the local rotated A/C tile equality. The complete inputs, script, and results are retained as V01.

The historical archive was independently decoded and all 8,024 reconstructed source texts matched their declared normalized or literal-text hashes. This verifies the preservation process. It does not mean every historical experiment was rerun, every original binary file was recovered, or every interpretation in those records is correct. Later chapters identify results supported by prior receipts rather than silently calling them new execution results.

The archive's original-file hashes remain useful provenance. Where the old master preserved an anonymized or normalized text representation of a DOCX, log, or source file, the reconstruction verifies that representation. The collection does not mislabel it as the original binary container. This distinction lets the research remain both readable and honest about what has survived.

# Part I — The original puzzle and the historical investigation

This part explains how the original treasure was solved, why investigation continued, and what the historical and cryptographic branches actually established. It consolidates the results retained in the October 13 baseline. The bracketed references identify the original baseline sections; they also distinguish preserved experimental receipts from experiments performed for a later edition. Later chapters govern developments after this cutoff. An earlier open question is not silently treated as current when a later test has answered it.

The central result is substantial: the published Bitcoin construction contains an authenticated decryption that completes a public twelve-word mnemonic, and that mnemonic derives the account receiving the documented Zenon prize allocation. Further numerical, visual, literary and signature relationships also reproduce. Their status depends on whether the rule selecting them is established, not merely whether their arithmetic works. Keeping those distinctions visible preserves the useful work without turning provisional links into historical facts. (B:Authenticated primary solve; B:Evidence language; B:Current proof gaps and supersession rules)

## 1. The original object is a transaction construction

The starting artifact is a set of linked Bitcoin transactions. A funding transaction creates nineteen staging outputs. Nineteen subsequent transactions each publish one line of artwork and leave a spendable output. A terminal transaction spends those nineteen outputs in the order that defines the picture. This gives a native, recoverable ordering: the image is not dependent on the order in which an explorer happens to display transactions. Each published row is exactly 38 ASCII bytes, giving nineteen rows and 722 artwork cells. (B:Authenticated primary solve; B:K34; B:K35)

| Construction component | Retained native result | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| Funding | Block 709594; nineteen staging outputs of 75,000 satoshis | Establishes the common construction and the inputs available to the row transactions |
| Nineteen rows | Block 709632; each output 0 contains 38 ASCII bytes | Provides the exact artwork, including spaces and punctuation |
| Row outputs and fees | Nineteen outputs of 69,240 satoshis; 5,760 satoshis fee per row transaction | Preserves the complete amount pattern without presuming it is another code |
| Terminal | Block 709632; spends the row outputs in row order | Fixes the assembly and publishes the literal project name |
| Other fees | Funding fee 25,000; terminal fee 1,008,000 satoshis | Separates ordinary transaction accounting from later numerical hypotheses |
| Core signatures | Forty P2WPKH/SIGHASH_ALL events verifying under Q | Authenticates the Bitcoin construction in its actual signing domain |
| Later sweep | Two further Q signature events | Creates a separate 42-event population; it must not be confused with the original forty |

These are SegWit-v0 transactions using BIP143 signature hashing. Taproot supplies the activation setting for the puzzle; it does not change these particular spends into Taproot spends. Uniform transaction version 2, funding locktime 709593, and non-final sequences have ordinary protocol functions. A proposed hidden channel in these fields has to demonstrate actual varying, author-controlled information. Their presence alone is insufficient. (B:Authenticated primary solve; B:What the steganography book audit actually adds)

The terminal output script is `6a0d5a454e4f4e204e4554574f524b`. Its payload is the thirteen ASCII bytes `ZENON NETWORK`. All nineteen terminal signatures commit to this output. The Bitcoin-to-Zenon connection therefore begins with a literal signed construction, before any literary interpretation or dictionary arithmetic. The [funding transaction](https://blockstream.info/tx/dd3bbc563197a6cddc78ad4a432dc92ba9c207809fc10db7d2716937d27c5d4d) and [terminal transaction](https://blockstream.info/tx/911dcb7435932f64215f8de4058186aef9bfd4356978c95830e77a38b9484083) are the preserved public references. (B:Authenticated primary solve)

The exact row strings are retained in the constants and raw evidence volumes. Two serialization lengths must remain distinct: the bare artwork is 722 bytes; adding an LF after every row produces 741 bytes. The latter has SHA256 `96534d9faf3b6260987939fdb6994cd5b97eed18a175b0620c4ea5256c0c5607`. Trailing spaces belong to the source. A screenshot, a rewrapped code block, or a copied image is a presentation of that source rather than a substitute for its bytes. (B:K35)

### 1.1 Four fields complete the seed

Four Base64 substrings are embedded in the artwork. Coordinates below use zero-based rows and half-open column intervals: `[7:31)` includes columns 7 through 30. Decoding is performed independently for each field. (B:Authenticated primary solve; B:K35)

| Field | Location | Base64 text | Decoded role and length |
|---|---|---|---|
| A | Row 0, columns `[7:31)` | `BynQtpeUyWTXKGTrGhdV2Q==` | Nonce, 16 bytes |
| B | Row 1, columns `[7:31)` | `tVMd3L1CKM4wFmyxEEEUV2bY` | First ciphertext segment, 18 bytes |
| C | Row 2, columns `[7:15)` | `4Fdzw1k=` | Second ciphertext segment, 5 bytes |
| E | Row 8, columns `[7:31)` | `vtv3f5aKY0jGQglP9a1AGw==` | Authentication tag, 16 bytes |

The decryption uses AES-256-GCM, with a key equal to SHA256 of the exact ASCII text `Taproot`, nonce A, ciphertext B followed by C, tag E, and no additional authenticated data. It produces:

```text
wall nation drive smoke
```

GCM matters because it checks more than whether a candidate output looks like English. Its authentication tag checks the ciphertext under the specified key and nonce. The successful decryption is the firmest decoding step in the solve. It authenticates this completion in its cryptographic domain; it does not authenticate every later interpretation of the same bytes. (B:Authenticated primary solve)

The public eight-word prefix and the recovered four-word completion form:

```text
oblige dilemma hurry disorder happy spoil shiver key wall nation drive smoke
```

This is public puzzle data. Its full twelve-word form passes the BIP39 checksum. The eight-word prefix, normalized to single spaces with no terminal newline, has SHA256 `e0468f21f2640a0a01a30a864d115bda60ce686eedc225b228625cb4cac463dd`. Keeping the prefix and completion separate preserves their different provenance: one was publicly disclosed; the other was recovered through authenticated decryption. (B:Authenticated primary solve)

### 1.2 The mnemonic reaches the documented prize

The accepted derivation uses an empty mnemonic passphrase and the native hardened Ed25519 path `m/44'/73404'/0'`. Zenon's SHA3-256 and Bech32 encoding then produces:

```text
z1qrn3jeapt848zxg3akf2ewhrxxwsa945sj798s
```

In the pinned official genesis configuration, this is a unique typed match at `GenesisBlocks.Blocks[9190].Address`, among 14,090 account records. Its allocation is 474,513,400,000 raw QSR units, equivalent to 4,745.134 QSR. The retained audit reproduces the native account-block hashes and verifies the height-2 Ed25519 signature under the derived quest key. The [pinned SDK](https://github.com/zenon-network/znn_sdk_dart/tree/26810aff625475d5e0ca6d703b7104224b5f396a/lib/src/wallet), [pinned genesis configuration](https://github.com/zenon-network/go-zenon/blob/aee7ab6e0874b91b29c72226f5da8cfb83218449/chain/genesis/embedded_genesis_string.go), and [quest preservation copy](https://forum.hypercore.one/t/12-seed-word-treasure-hunt/421) anchor this result. (B:Authenticated primary solve)

There are several different authorities here. Bitcoin Q authenticates the original transaction construction. The derived quest key authenticates its Zenon account actions. An unsigned genesis record and a node's reported inclusion are not additional Q signatures, and reproducing selected native objects is narrower than independently validating an entire consensus chain. Those qualifications leave the original treasure derivation intact. (B:Authenticated primary solve)

The retained burn records close a further part of the account history. The send `ac3fb28796c0aec1c512396e54f23fe5882246c44372cc23897d455cfe88f7eb` was confirmed on 23 December 2021 at 14:55:10 UTC; its receive `c65cfdc5db8818729f19acea9289a965c0c5e95b1e15289700cce7b484f35272` followed at 14:56:10 UTC. It burns the 4,745.134 QSR allocation and contains no scripted continuation. A signature under a now-public puzzle mnemonic does not uniquely identify which person used it. (B:Parked infrastructure and settlement findings retain their narrower scope)

### 1.3 The block anchor and the prefix disclosure have separate histories

The configuration's ExtraData is exactly:

```text
000000000000000000004dd040595540d43ce8ff5946eeaa403fb13d0e582d8f#We are all Satoshi#Don't trust. Verify
```

The first component names Bitcoin block 710968. Its retained 80-byte header reproduces the double-SHA256 block hash and encoded proof-of-work target. The two phrases are genuine project-source data. The pinned release commit is dated 22 January 2022; the file's contents alone do not establish public availability of that final configuration in November 2021. (B:Authenticated primary solve)

Later source recovery corrected the origin of the eight-word screenshot. Native metadata identifies [community post 1460002976907595779](https://twitter.com/crypto_culture_/status/1460002976907595779), dated 14 November 2021 at 21:53:29 UTC, containing the exact eight words. It has no native attached image. Screenshot line wrapping therefore cannot be treated as an issuer-designed spatial alphabet. The original issuer-controlled first/middle-four disclosure, and the original links from a deleted first post, were still missing in the baseline. The source correction narrows what the screenshot can prove; it does not undo the successful mnemonic derivation. (B:The preserved eight-word image has a community Twitter origin)

## 2. Why investigation continued after the treasure

The next clue was numerical. BIP39 assigns each English word an index from 0 to 2047, so a word can represent eleven bits. The accepted mnemonic's final entropy byte is 102, whose word is ART. Subtracting 102 from the four recovered word indices, modulo 2048, gives:

```text
WALL NATION DRIVE SMOKE
          minus ART
TRY  MAKE   CUSTOM SCALE
```

The arithmetic is exact and uses the authenticated completion. Reading the result as an invitation to investigate another scale is reasonable. The unresolved question is which scale, which input, and which next operation the source establishes. (B:K04)

### 2.1 The older word machine is a conditional model

The older proposal subtracts ART across all twelve mnemonic positions and treats the resulting ordered vector as a program. Its first label is MERGE. The later audit separated three steps that earlier narratives had run together: producing a vector, showing that it is executable code, and choosing its initial instruction. MERGE is an English label until a rule makes it an operation. A `4|4|4` arrangement is an ordered organization of data until a consumer uses it as code. (B:K04)

The final four positions reproduce TRY MAKE CUSTOM SCALE because those four positions supplied the original transformation. They are training examples. Reproducing them is a useful consistency check, but not an independent forty-four-bit prediction. This matters because later branches can be perfectly reproducible while remaining conditional on this initial program interpretation. (B:K04)

Within the installed model, operations include addition, both subtraction directions, XOR, AND and OR. Addition and subtraction wrap modulo 2048; the bitwise operations act on eleven-bit values. A state can carry an operation, and that operation can affect the subsequent path. Thus `BEGIN[ADD]` and `BEGIN[AND]` are different occurrences, despite sharing the same word. A checksum repair called CHECK changes the checksum part of a candidate representation; it does not by itself authorize a new program, source or identity claim. (B:K07; B:K10; B:K34)

The graph became large: one retained archive has 8,120 definitions and 113,990 anchors. Those counts measure an installed research model, not thousands of independently authenticated puzzle instructions. A reentry study makes the distinction concrete: of 2,445 reentries, 300 retained the operation, 2,145 changed it, and 2,115 changed the complete path. No provenance transfers or restored parent histories were recorded. Reaching the same label again does not reconstruct a completed earlier join. (B:K10)

### 2.2 The forty-word table is exact source data

Concatenating the decoded bytes in artwork order—A, then B, then C, then E—gives 55 bytes, or 440 bits. That is exactly forty eleven-bit values, with no remainder. This source concatenation is different from the argument order used by AES-GCM. Displayed as five rows of eight, the values are: (B:K05; B:K35)

| Positions | Eight words in source order |
|---|---|
| 0–7 | ALREADY · EXCESS · COLOR · CONNECT · ERODE · RAVEN · FREQUENT · ARRIVE |
| 8–15 | TWELVE · SPAWN · STEP · RECALL · FESTIVAL · MOMENT · INDICATE · PORTION |
| 16–23 | CHURN · IMMENSE · AIRPORT · SUNNY · BABY · CALM · EYEBROW · UNDER |
| 24–31 | SUGGEST · ACTOR · TABLE · MAMMAL · ORBIT · HOSPITAL · TARGET · VERSION |
| 32–39 | MEDIA · BOTTOM · COVER · LOTTERY · NEUTRAL · WIDTH · RELAX · ADDRESS |

The conspicuous final column contains UNDER, VERSION and ADDRESS, and the last row contains several plausible instructions. The table is therefore worth preserving exactly. Its eight-column display is a proposed organization of forty values, however; it is not the artwork's physical width of 38 characters. (B:K05)

Decoding each field separately gives a different boundary structure. A provides eleven words and seven remaining bits `1011001`; B provides thirteen words and one remaining bit `0`; C provides three words and seven remaining bits `1011001`; E provides eleven words and seven remaining bits `0011011`. The twenty-two residual bits concatenate to RAZOR/NATURE. These are residuals of the eleven-bit reading, not unused AES data. The field-local and concatenated views reuse the same bytes and cannot be counted as independent witnesses. (B:K05; B:K35)

### 2.3 The source-local punctuation proposal remains distinct

A later proposal derives an ordered four-symbol alphabet from punctuation fields and their own attached suffixes. It addresses CUSTOM SCALE without needing the whole older program interpretation. The baseline retains it as a source-local avenue with its own tests and limitations. The physical chapters give its complete construction and subsequent developments. It does not retroactively validate every descendant of the older interpreter, and a later stronger physical result must not be used to rewrite the provenance of an older historical branch. (B:K04; B:K12; B:Current proof gaps and supersession rules)

## 3. Q authenticates the construction; historical points require separate selection

The construction public key is serialized directly in the witnesses:

```text
Q = 0398cf668fe678cc6d8e2b20b5f574c9f398c7f031a1e91e9b85029e4cde597ad7
```

It matches the P2WPKH commitments and verifies the native signatures. This is stronger than discovering a suggestive dictionary label in a coordinate: Q's relationship to the construction is a protocol fact. (B:K09; B:K35)

An ECDSA signature can also be used to enumerate candidate public keys once the correct message digest is known. In the retained terminal study, each of nineteen signatures has two recovery candidates. The proposed pair 11/18 intersects at Q, but so do all 171 distinct terminal pairs. Across the forty core signatures, all 780 distinct pairs likewise isolate Q. Recovery demonstrates the verification relationship; choosing a special-looking pair does not make Q an exceptional hidden discovery. (B:K09; B:K14)

The proposed 11/18 selector uses two representations of a public recovery-gap point: raw DESIGN478 and normalized CLIMB344. Division by forty gives quotient 11 and remainder 38; division by nineteen gives quotient 18 and remainder 2. Those computations reproduce, but the choice of representation and divisor remains part of the selector's contract. English usefulness after division cannot choose the rule retroactively. (B:K09)

### 3.1 P2095 and P3928 are not interchangeable

Two historical Bitcoin public points recur in the record. P2095 is the older historical point. P3928 is the corrected point obtained from input 145 of the retained 205-input consolidation, through a route associated with the block 3928 coinbase. Each creates a different public difference point:

```text
R2095 = Q − P2095
R3928 = Q − P3928
```

Public elliptic-curve subtraction is legitimate arithmetic. It does not demonstrate knowledge of either corresponding private scalar. Substituting one P or R for the other changes the object, its reductions, and its ancestry. Both complete SEC encodings remain in the constants volume. (B:K07; B:K08; B:K35)

The historical consolidation is transaction `29591d211054478ed22c93571955cb92ef4ec2b40d914a43ba3a91d52a171c61`. Its native list has 205 ordered, zero-based inputs. The retained raw transaction is 23,446 bytes, SHA256 `24f2ff30597e2821e83f7c42a7f9c5d0b144f5f509364ba21e49139f22f4abe6`. In the older route, the block 2905 coinbase outpoint identifies input 134; nonce-coordinate reductions give ACQUIRE/ADD; GLOBAL40's RECALL at position 11 supplies the proposed offset to input 145. The spent P2PK output there supplies P3928. (B:K08; B:Historical205-input pointers and the Entry39 alternatives)

The native historical transaction and public points are genuine. The original punctuation packing used to select block 2905 was later superseded. Consequently this remains a conditional route to genuine public objects, rather than the established continuation of the corrected punctuation scale. Keeping that status avoids the false choice between discarding real ledger data and treating every route to it as proven. (B:K08)

### 3.2 The complete P3928/Q/R3928 reduction census

A coordinate census begins with an exact 32-byte coordinate, produces its canonical BIP39 representation, and tests eighteen fixed reductions: three pairings times six operations. Every checksum-valid result is retained with its endpoint and checksum. The six coordinates of P3928, Q and R3928 produced these nine valid results; P3928.x produced none. (B:K08; B:K34)

| Coordinate | Pairing and operation | Endpoint | Checksum |
|---|---|---|---:|
| P3928.y | Adjacent, right minus left | ASSAULT108 | 14 |
| P3928.y | Mirror, ADD | AFFAIR34 | 0 |
| Q.x | Halves, OR | BUYER251 | 9 |
| Q.y | Mirror, OR | ART102 | 9 |
| Q.y | Halves, ADD | ABOVE4 | 7 |
| R3928.x | Adjacent, ADD | ASPECT107 | 12 |
| R3928.x | Adjacent, OR | ALTER59 | 6 |
| R3928.y | Adjacent, ADD | AGENT39 | 13 |
| R3928.y | Halves, left minus right | ATTEND116 | 1 |

This is a complete result within a declared finite family. It demonstrates the arithmetic and checksum outcomes. It does not show that the dictionary meanings select a CALL definition, or that a four-bit checksum is a historical signature. Q's ART/ABOVE and BUYER relationships are retained without upgrading them into an identity assertion. (B:K08; B:K09)

## 4. What the P2095 compiler branch accomplished

The P2095 branch deserves a connected account because it contains real, reproducible work that later strict audits qualified. The August 28 reports began from the numbered-poem route, an ACTOR poem-line operand, P2095, and R2095. They assumed the whole-state six-operation CUSTOM SCALE and the CHECK normalizer. Later auditing did not validate that boot as an unconditional instruction from the root. Every result below therefore retains this historical compiler as an explicit dependency. (B:K07)

### 4.1 Dispatch and the ENVELOPE connection

The scale outputs were ADD→AISLE45, left-minus-right→ADVANCE31, right-minus-left→ABLE2, XOR→ABUSE9, AND→ADMIT29, and OR→ACTUAL23. Using the compressed R object as twenty-four complete eleven-bit cells gave the following retained dispatch. (B:K07)

| Selector | Quotient on division by 24 | Selected position | Label there | Carried result |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|
| AISLE45 | 1 | 21 | DENY | DOCTOR |
| ADVANCE31 | 1 | 7 | FIRE | PROUD |
| ABLE2 | 0 | 2 | MERIT | MERCY |
| ABUSE9 | 0 | 9 | STING | STATE |
| ADMIT29 | 1 | 5 | PAVE | ACCOUNT |
| ACTUAL23 | 0 | 23 | ENERGY | ENOUGH |

Packing the six quotients gives binary `110010`, decimal 50, ALIEN. The report uses ENERGY with ALIEN to produce EXPECT and then FINAL, with contextual FILE/FINISH checks. Division is mechanical; packing the quotients and promoting them to a state are further rules of this compiler. They are not a general license to reinterpret any residual from a different branch. (B:K07)

For P2095.x, the eighteen pair/operator reductions have two CHECK-valid rows: adjacent/AND ending ANCHOR67 and mirror/ADD ending BONUS203. Their retained concatenation and normalization gives endpoint ANOTHER. The proposed connector installs BONUS into the final value of the mirrored R2095.x ADD result, appends the EXPECT checkpoint, and CHECK-normalizes the result. The checked foot becomes ENVELOPE and the entropy endpoint becomes BEGIN. These are separate outputs with different subsequent consumers. (B:K07)

The accompanying controls are useful. Arbitrary final-slot replacements across eighteen reductions and all 2,048 anchors give 167 ENVELOPE hits among 36,864 candidates. Requiring the same P.x pairing, operation and endpoint to be carried to R.x gives one hit among eighteen: mirror/ADD/BONUS. These numbers describe exactly what was compared. They do not supply a probability that the puzzle author intended the compiler, because the installation and appended checkpoint remain assumed steps. (B:K07)

The checked ENVELOPE foot was routed using several proposed divisors, including ACCOUNT12, 26 and 22. One retained route gives FESTIVAL and then `ENVELOPE − FESTIVAL = WALL`, returning to an authenticated root value. The BEGIN endpoint, carrying ADD, follows a longer path through AGAIN, EDGE, ANGER, BLOOD, LITTLE, CUSHION, DIARY, FADE, BODY, AUTHOR and DEBRIS to AREA. Returning to a known authenticated word is a recognizable loop; it does not authenticate every edge used to get there. (B:K07)

### 4.2 Input 6 and public verifier points

A separate consumer uses selectors as native byte offsets in 32-byte coordinates. AISLE45 is out of range and is excluded without a modulo repair. The remaining native byte order is `[2,9,23,29,31]`, whereas scale-generation order is `[31,2,9,29,23]`. The source retained both orders rather than using the decoded words to choose one. (B:K07)

At terminal input 6, the two relevant signature-coordinate byte positions yield BULLET/CABBAGE and AROUND/ATTEND. Cross-applying the operations associated with selectors 2 and 9 gives ACCOUNT at position 2 under both tested operations, ACT at position 9 under right-minus-left, and ACTOR at position 9 under XOR. The resulting arithmetic fingerprint is unique among the nineteen terminal witnesses. Cross-applying two operators as a two-by-two fanout remains a conditional instruction, even though its result is selective. (B:K07)

The public point construction uses the native signature values `r`, `s` and digest `z`, with `u1=z/s` and `u2=r/s` modulo the curve order. It then forms `A=u1G`, `C=u2P2095`, `D=u2R2095`, `K=A+u2Q`, and `V=A+D=K−C`. These are all publicly computable verification objects. This A is a local point name and must not be confused with the artwork's nonce field A. No private scalar is recovered. (B:K07)

Under the retained uniform-AND interpretation, V.x/V.y at the five selected positions return ABANDON, ACCOUNT, ABOVE, BEGIN and ABILITY in native order. A census of nineteen witnesses, five points and seven procedures gives 665 candidates; one matches the entire target and the same candidate matches its first/last pair. The competing per-selector operator carry instead returns VISA, BLUR, ARROW, BEGIN and VOCAL. These are different contracts, not two executions of the same instruction. (B:K07)

All child outcomes were kept, including cycles. ABANDON/AND remains ABANDON; ACCOUNT/AND reaches ABLE; ABOVE/AND cycles through ACTRESS; BEGIN/AND cycles through ABSURD and ACOUSTIC; ABILITY/AND enters the ABOVE/ACTRESS cycle. BEGIN/AND cannot be spliced onto the BEGIN/ADD route. Nor do nonadjacent ABANDON and ABILITY automatically form a call-zero instruction. (B:K07)

A later report joins three historical parents at ENVELOPE/ADD and follows ENVELOPE→KISS→BETRAY. Its installed BETRAY/AND handler emits BOOK/ABANDON; the old call-zero handler maps zero through cosine to ABILITY. Two complete thirty-seven-state rounds reproduce the same states, operations, external requests, output and acknowledgement, without new requests. This closes an earlier question inside that model. It does not establish its CALL-zero handler or restore the rejected first/last-word shortcut. (B:K07)

## 5. Native software execution taught an important boundary

A decoded label can name a real source concept without selecting a callable method. The go-zenon audit illustrates this. The retained commit `1baa7c4e057da4f2708a970b4fedf70a8de77fbe` contains 240 Go files and a fifty-five-term census. Its date is 23 November 2021, nine days after the artwork. Its merge and join functions operate on typed protocol or software objects. They do not accept seven BIP39 cells merely because the source contains MERGE, NETWORK or ZENON. The sole seven-argument ABI method, RegisterLegacy, lacks both a selected destination/method and valid argument types at the proposed root. (B:K10)

The selected call 1024 has what that root proposal lacks: an actual destination, ABI selector `47f12c81`, two typed RetrieveAssets fields, and native links. Its complete trace is the user's send, Swap receipt and Mint child, Token receipt and transfer, and the original caller's signed receive. The transfer is 469,136,612 raw ZNN units. Six hashes, two Ed25519 signatures and legacy compact recovery verify in the retained audit, with thirty-two controls. Final data and descendants are empty. The native settlement is complete without returning another puzzle opcode. (B:K10)

Under the older TABLE/JUDGE hypothesis, the four projections of public ordinal 1024 are CALL/ARTWORK/ARCTIC/ADULT, a unique satisfying fingerprint. The old continuation promoted the first field to an opcode and the second to call 105. Source genealogy found this promotion earlier than the WALK/LENGTH justification. It was therefore not a prospectively established adapter. A useful fingerprint and a successfully settled native call can both be real while that continuation fails. (B:K10)

Two later deliveries reinforce the distinction. The selected MOUNTAIN operation was followed to a signed receive with empty data and zero descendants. The ARTIST-related CALL104 authorizes an ordinary 2.35985 ZNN swap, matches genesis entry 2470, and reaches a newly authenticated height-2 receive at `z1qztc5y325kf5nawlzfy63yd939cn6j2u9cm7qp`. Its exact amount, destination and paired links pass; altered digest/preimage controls fail. Its final data is also empty. These receipts close their selected settlement routes; none is a new Q relationship or historical attribution statement. (B:The145-address board, GAS closure and object-bound evidence test; B:Entry 123, collective-key controls and native ARTIST delivery)

Terminal input 18 is another example of preserving the object before interpreting its labels. The raw-s object is the exact 32-byte, unnormalized DER s scalar, read as twenty-three eleven-bit cells plus three residual bits. P08 is BROOM, P05 QUANTUM and P12 INSTALL. QUANTUM and INSTALL coincide with already named source-local phrase positions in the conditional research. BROOM has no carried operation or member selector. It cannot authorize sweeping an arbitrary database simply because that is a natural English reading. (B:K24)

## 6. External works were investigated as possible instructions and proof analogies

The external branches are not disposable decoration. They preserve the reasoning that connected the puzzle to original/copy distinctions, signatures, visible demonstrations and the difference between a creator and a character. Their evidentiary role must be stated individually. A work can be correctly identified while its proposed return to the puzzle remains unearned. (B:K06; B:K28)

| Source family | What the retained work established | Boundary that remains |
|---|---|---|
| *Many Facez* | The 1997 release, seventeen-track edition and contemporary five-persona description support the retained media interpretation | Choosing editions, LOTTERY divisors, operators and parent return needs the relevant local rule |
| Rush promotional pair | Earthshine and One Little Victory fit the retained EARTH/GLARE and VICTORY/AMOUNT interpretation | GLARE→SHINE is semantic, disc order differs, and targets/operands were already known |
| Numbered poem and letter 439 | The selected J1336/F1371 poem, manuscript/catalogue context and “little Book” editorial note are recoverable | Edition-specific counts and an editorial conjecture do not become source-native commands |
| *Sevenoaks* | The selected text contains identity recognition, original letters, signature comparison, demonstration and record | Narrative correspondence does not identify a controller of a historical private key |
| *Yellow Woman* | Author/character names match selected whitepaper references conditionally | Name matches do not establish a reference-number dispatcher or transfer identities |
| *The Endless* | The retained synopsis/interview set supplies the sixteen tested film words and predecessor-title context | Proper names remain proper names; character and historical person are not interchangeable |
| Nehemiah | Walls, rebuilding, books, census and missing genealogy support a proof-themed reading | Numeric or narrative parallels do not prove identity |
| Hashcash first-cut source | Package and detached signature can authenticate that source's author | That authority is not automatically Satoshi or Q |

For *Many Facez*, the five-by-six table-operator census found a unique bottom-row AND result passing the installed checksum. It was tempting to read NEUTRAL as a license for AND, but that argument followed observation. The main TABLE/JUDGE acceptance also preceded the sibling census. Later corrections rejected the claim that depth-first audit order itself established a return barrier. The album remains a genuine external object; the stronger control-flow interpretation is conditional. Preserved references include the [edition listing](https://recordsale.de/en/artists/tracey-lee/albums/many-facez) and [contemporary review](https://www.washingtonpost.com/archive/lifestyle/1997/06/04/lees-facez-in-a-crowd/436415ac-664b-4ee4-b1db-4adc9af6d291/). (B:K06)

The Rush arithmetic `RIGHT1486 − DEGREE462 = LENGTH1024` is exact. Reversing it modulo 2048 gives the same result because 1024 is half the modulus; that is not a second independent confirmation. The retained [promotional-disc reference](https://www.cygnus-x1.net/links/rush/single-onelittlevictory-earthshine.php), [Earthshine](https://www.rush.com/songs/earthshine/) and [One Little Victory](https://www.rush.com/songs/one-little-victory/) can acknowledge an already pending LENGTH interpretation. They do not establish a generic media-return rule. (B:K06)

The poem route has an exact numerical attraction: `710968 − 709632 = 1336`, corresponding to the retained numbered poem. Its [poem](https://archive.emilydickinson.org/correspondence/holland/p1336.html), [letter](https://archive.emilydickinson.org/correspondence/holland/l439.html), [editorial note](https://archive.emilydickinson.org/correspondence/holland/jnl439.html), and [manuscript catalogue](https://hollisarchives.lib.harvard.edu/catalog/hou01534_hou01534c00039) remain separate sources. The retained transcription lengths 24/21/30/20 sum to 95, ARMOR. Nearby poems also yield dense puzzle vocabulary, so the successful count is not strong enough by itself to select an edition and character-count convention. (B:K28)

The chosen [Sevenoaks text](https://www.gutenberg.org/cache/epub/15214/pg15214.txt) has SHA256 `94750ec4ab19b081c4eab43a286bd666fb3a05b5049bc34f79cf801a835baf5f`. Its thirty chapters provide a coherent proof-themed narrative. Retained numerical reductions of 10,000,000 and 500 give SOURCE1664 and DIRECT500, whose conditional sum returns ATTEND116. These are preserved correspondences, not a cryptographic signature by the person the research ultimately sought to identify. (B:K28)

The four-video audit inspected containers, timelines, contact sheets, audio spectra and public metadata. It found ordinary H264/AAC presentation and no obvious subtitle, attachment or data-stream payload yielding a signature, key or transaction hash. Later recovery added genuine promotional, rollout and wallet-transfer footage. The December 23 burn video's 600,064 decoded audio samples were all zero, with independent controls. None of this is a universal proof that no steganography exists; it records the tested observable channels and the absence of a new literal instruction in them. (B:K06; B:Other long-horizon source recoveries and finite closures)

## 7. The exact message and its 145 signatures

The 2020 publication contributes a different authenticated object: an exact 362-byte message and 145 compact Bitcoin message signatures. The message uses four paragraphs separated by LF LF, no enclosing quotation marks, and no final newline. Its SHA256 is `bead321c006c7bfc62ab1cd9f585cb1138cbfe7cc78d419411ecc9949cfc033d`; its native Bitcoin Signed Message digest is `c4259881ceecf395c853020810dd6d5a6334ace42703f86464d3df58bac6c34e`. (B:K26)

All 145 compact signatures verify those exact bytes. Frozen quoted/newline and other variants do not. This verification uses Bitcoin's message framing, compact recovery, public-key serialization and address comparison. Hashing the text with raw SHA256 and calling that the same test would be an error. The signatures prove control of the respective signing authorities for this message. They do not prove 145 separate people, a particular civil identity, or Q's control of any of those keys. (B:K26)

The address publication also provides an ordered carrier for the word-board experiments. Removing each Base58 version byte leaves twenty HASH160 bytes and four checksum bytes. A fixed inverse-LSB reading down the 145 records partitions each column into thirteen eleven-bit words and a two-bit trailer, producing the retained thirteen-by-twenty-four board. The signatures authenticate the message, not the order of the list. The order has separate publication provenance. The earlier claim of an independently authenticated artwork entrance into that external publication was withdrawn. (B:The145-address board, GAS closure and object-bound evidence test)

### 7.1 Entry 123 and the tagged attribution proposal

One packet, displayed as Entry 123, produces a selective collection of equations, including TEAM AND SEEK=SEARCH, PATROL−TEAM=SECRET, VALID−PINK=ENVELOPE, and SEEK+RECORD=ITEM. Later reading preserves terminal fields `11|30` and `0|123`, corresponding to RECORD/AUTHOR. Replacing the first payload while retaining tag 11 yields `11|123`, SATOSHI1531. The operands have a source-local explanation; assigning that replacement and choosing a consumer remain conditional. (B:Tagged attribution and the Entry123 certificate; B:Entry 123, collective-key controls and native ARTIST delivery)

The complete fixed population has 145 packets and 1,595 adjacent pairs. Exactly one packet has terminal tags 11/0: Entry 123, raw-checksum valid and self-addressing under displayed one-based numbering. Five pairs qualify anywhere; the other four fail raw checksum. Zero-based self-reference has no match. These counts quantify the observed specificity. They are not a blind historical attribution proof, and displayed Entry 123 must not be silently converted into zero-based input 123 of another transaction. (B:Entry 123, collective-key controls and native ARTIST delivery)

The message's final phrase `We are all Satoshi` occupies bytes `[344:362)`, with its sole Satoshi token at `[355:362)`. A signature authenticates that assertion under the selected key. It does not bind that key to Q or certify the statement as a civil-identity claim. The later 145-key collective test is negative: adding all signer public keys does not produce Q, and neither does the standard empty-tree Taproot tweak of that sum. All 145 leave-one-out sums also miss Q. These fixed relationships are excluded; ordinary point addition is not a secure multisignature protocol. (B:Entry 123, collective-key controls and native ARTIST delivery)

### 7.2 Entry 39, nonce coordinates and recovery alternatives

Entry 39 uses address `12CTHhyJtr49LgoUShbWgebLBviLAFj6nj`. Its compact header fixes a public effective nonce point K satisfying the native ECDSA equation. Among its eighteen nonce-x reductions, one is checksum-valid: HALVES/right-minus-left. It yields POND SHORT DISH BARGAIN IMPOSE INPUT LUNAR TOGETHER COMIC OFTEN LOCAL PATROL, endpoint ANXIETY80 and checksum 9. Across all 145 nonce coordinates, 166 of 2,610 reductions validate, and fifty-seven records have exactly one valid lane. Entry 39 alone has the frozen combined signer/nonce tuple. (B:Historical205-input pointers and the Entry39 alternatives)

A million random coordinate-pair controls gave sixty-eight signer hits, seventeen nonce hits, two sole-valid nonce hits, and zero joint hits. The marginal expected joint count was only 0.001156, so zero joint hits adds little evidence. These random coordinate pairs were not valid ECDSA records and do not provide an authorship probability. The distinction prevents a dramatic-looking zero from being used beyond its test. (B:Historical205-input pointers and the Entry39 alternatives)

The alternative-recovery study forms GAP as accepted public key minus the opposite recovery candidate. Its public relation `GAP=(2s/r)K` verifies for all 145 records. Entry 39 has a sole raw33 HALVES/OR reduction ending ANTENNA78, checksum 15 and foot OVER. Only that entry has endpoint 78 in the frozen census. The appealing “command followed by the next two cells” reading was proposed after observing the result. DENY SOURCE rejects unreliable attribution while retaining the exact signed witness; it does not select the opposite recovery candidate or import P3928's procedure. (B:Historical205-input pointers and the Entry39 alternatives)

### 7.3 Direct historical-key joins failed

The conditional word selector found input 145 alone satisfying its predeclared BONUS/MESSAGE conjunction across the 205-input historical transaction; four alternative digest populations had no such conjunction. Native identity verification gave a different answer: the 205 distinct historical authorities and 145 message authorities have zero key intersection. Five frozen digest variants cover 148,625 comparison cells, all zero; a 10,000-digest null family also has no joins. These close the tested equality/recovery predicates. Empty recovery intersections are not positive evidence. (B:K26)

A separate pointer walk illustrates a clean stop. Historical input 87's fixed MIRROR/right-minus-left reducer gives endpoint APOLOGY83, which the existing same-population map consumes as input 83. Input 83 retains endpoint BUILD238 after CHECK repairs only its four checksum bits. Since 238 lies outside the valid input range 0–204, the native pointer walk stops. The later BUILD→CALL→ANOTHER→TEST/XOR→SATOSHI route requires a new reducer-to-CALL admission. Its readability cannot turn 238 into a valid historical input. (B:Historical205-input pointers and the Entry39 alternatives)

## 8. Natural signature channels were tested without finding a payload

The October 4 independent audit verified all 145 signatures with two implementations. All r values are unique and below `2^255`; all s values are low-S. These features are compatible with ordinary low-R/low-S signing. A pre-May-2020 Electrum low-R procedure offers a routine explanation, but does not prove which software signed this particular publication. Header parity and recovered effective nonce-point y parity are the same channel, not two independent bits of evidence. (B:Independent145-signature carrier and steganography audit)

The constrained channel search used the recovery header, the lowest one through eight bits of r and s, declared interleavings, forward/reverse record order, polarities and alignments. It did not permit arbitrary sorts, subsets, permutations, guessed decryption or an unrestricted XOR-key search. Its 4,160 labeled variants reduce to 3,880 distinct streams containing 504,664 bytes. None matches the 583 declared targets or validates as a file, whole DER object or whole printable UTF8 string. The longest ASCII run is twelve bytes. (B:Independent145-signature carrier and steganography audit)

| Framing test | Retained population | Authenticated hits |
|---|---:|---:|
| Distinct 32-byte scalar windows against signer x coordinates | 387,024 windows | 0 |
| Raw 64-byte ECDSA windows | 280,888 windows; 561,776 recovery attempts | 0 |
| Compact 65-byte windows | 277,840 | 0 |
| Embedded compressed-key/ECDSA frames | 3,280 | 0 |
| Embedded x-only-key/BIP340 frames | 387,742 | 0 |
| Binary address/WIF Base58Check attempts | 4,874 | 0 |

Valid scalar parsing and generic ECDSA recovery can occur in random data; they are not extracted signatures without a committed verifier and message. BIP340 against existing signer keys without an embedded key was not tested in that run, and external Q/native witness digests were not supplied. Those scope limits remain part of the result. (B:Independent145-signature carrier and steganography audit)

There was a parity imbalance worth recording: `header XOR r_LSB XOR s_LSB` has 91 zeros and 54 ones. A reproducible null using 2,000 sets of 145 accepted low-R/low-S signatures gives a plus-one probability of about 0.45% for that exact triple and about 1.60% for an equally extreme imbalance somewhere among seven tested combinations. The latter raw family frequency has a Wilson 95% interval of 1.09–2.19%. These values do not account for the broader research search, and are not the probability of steganography. No payload, key or witness was extracted. (B:Independent145-signature carrier and steganography audit)

## 9. The historical OpenPGP certificate is real; the proposed new claim is missing

The preserved historical public certificate is 1,714 bytes of ASCII armor, decoding to 1,159 packet bytes. Its primary fingerprint is:

```text
DE4EFCA3E1AB9E41CE96CECB18C09E865EC948A1
```

It contains a DSA signing primary, user ID, positive certification, ElGamal encryption subkey, and binding signature. Both existing signatures validate the certificate's internal relationships. There are no secret-key packets. The packet's asserted creation time is 30 October 2008 at 18:19:19 UTC. ASCII armor is a transport encoding; its CRC24 detects accidental corruption, and an armor Version header is outside the signed packet stream. An encryption subkey cannot substitute for the signing primary. (B:K25)

The three retained copies are byte-identical. References include [archived bitcoin.org](https://web.archive.org/web/20110228054007id_/http://www.bitcoin.org/Satoshi_Nakamoto.asc), the [Nakamoto Institute copy](https://satoshi.nakamotoinstitute.org/satoshinakamoto.asc), the [Bitcoin Forum copy](https://bitcointalk.org/Satoshi_Nakamoto.asc), and the [historical preservation post](https://bitcointalk.org/index.php?topic=458.msg5772#msg5772). The binary SHA256 is `4dc1d9f55a9be2b279d725bc83cd4fd7049fbfb32513872be6446dea8419cff4`. (B:K25; B:K35)

The native UID certification signs a 505-byte preimage; the subkey binding signs 970 bytes. Their preimages share the 421-byte framed primary and then diverge into their respective subject types. Crossed signatures fail. These are signature types `0x13` and `0x18`; neither is a `0x00` binary-document signature over Q or the exact 362-byte message. The UID packet's last byte labels AUTHOR123; the binding endpoint labels BUNKER243. Those endpoint correspondences are retained, while their native signature roles remain unchanged. (B:Historical PRIMARY: packet anatomy, missing payload and finite DSA tests)

The old proposal requested a new binary-document signature by this primary over exact362. Such a signature would be meaningful evidence of control of that credential. The strict audit did not locate an occurrence that co-addressed this key and this message as a new-signature demand. The 145 Bitcoin signatures already satisfy a literal reading of signing that message. Thus the historical-primary challenge remains a proposed conditional verification contract, rather than the proved single missing ending. (B:K25)

Finite candidate testing found no response. The DSA framing matrix totals 7,554 checks, combining 6,528 earlier checks and a 1,026-check extension. The extension used 171 valid pairs and six preimages; two implementations agreed on all 768 candidate records and the extension checks. Native certificate signatures supplied positive controls and trailer mutations supplied negatives. Binary signing retains 362 bytes; text signing converts six LF characters to CRLF, giving 368 bytes without a final newline. High160, low160, modulo-q and exact-raw32 hashing mappings remain distinct; rejected out-of-range values were not repaired after failure. The missing older runner is disclosed, so user-described omissions are recovery guidance rather than independently verified extra coverage. (B:Historical PRIMARY: packet anatomy, missing payload and finite DSA tests)

## 10. Email 260, source chronology and retrospective recognition

Email 260 is central to the historical branch because its preserved encrypted reply has striking field correspondences. Two streams must remain separate: Recipient A is 692 bytes; Recipient B is a 434-byte RSA control. Recipient A's first packet is an ElGamal public-key-encrypted session-key packet and its second is an integrity-protected encrypted-data packet. Its exact SHA256 is `a1efc74bcc5f052e2f230b12ee1bd26ae4de3a3eba02a1581b93dd6bb4d3cc78`. (B:Email260: exact native fields, chronology and conditional selection)

| Recipient A native field | Exact result | Dictionary label |
|---|---:|---|
| Second MPI endpoint / first packet body endpoint, offset 528 | 138 | BACON |
| Encrypted-data body endpoint, offset 691 | 43 | AIR |
| Complete stream length, including headers | 692 | FINAL |
| First MPI endpoint, offset 270 | 109 | ASSET |

These fields are real parser results. Recipient A alone matches all four within a nineteen-container comparison population. That population contains fifteen certificates, two detached signatures and only two encrypted messages; it cannot honestly be described as nineteen equivalent encrypted-message competitors. Wider counts of four AIR endpoints among 644 packets and five AIR occurrences among 679 merged objects also retain their own namespaces. (B:Email260: exact native fields, chronology and conditional selection)

Chronology changes the evidentiary weight. Complete armor was exposed on 27 August 2026 at 20:58:57 UTC. The first retained parse of endpoint 138 is at 22:08:01, with BACON recognition six seconds later. The first recovered exact ordered expression `BUNKER243 − ARTWORK105` appears on 7 October. No earlier original selector receipt was recovered. FINAL and the FINAL[XOR] path toward ASSET predated the cipher inspection, but CHOOSE AIR's trailer reading followed AIR's earlier recognition. The combined four-field result is therefore a mixture of earlier context and retrospective recognition, not a wholly anticipated independent prediction. That chronology neither proves deliberate fitting nor permits the stronger precommitment claim. (B:Email260: exact native fields, chronology and conditional selection)

The retained Q/ElGamal exponent comparison gives zero hits in 210 cases. Recipient A and the historical certificate use different public subkeys: their p and y values differ even though g agrees. A full 256-byte MPI does not become an admitted 32-byte entropy scalar simply because some metadata endpoints fit the dictionary. No full-MPI consumer was recovered. The earliest independently anchored exact public ciphertext copy is dated 23 February 2024; bounded pre-2021/pre-2022 searches found none, which is an availability finding rather than universal absence or exclusive possession. (B:Email260: exact native fields, chronology and conditional selection)

The late October 7 audit therefore demoted AIR/ACTUAL to a conditional research route. The first missing entrance is the selection of BUNKER and ARTWORK, their order, subtraction and comparison domain before matching the ciphertext. Even granting that step, AIR43 alone does not provide CHOOSE322 or an ADD register. Native mailbox recovery would improve source custody, but does not automatically repair either missing instruction. (B:Cross-object negatives and the controlling current boundary)

The existing downstream model is still preserved. A fresh ADD closure has 1,864 templates, 7,395 role edges, 5,592 side/join path checks and 1,864 installed join-arithmetic checks. It has no native SATOSHI or OUTPUT state, output or join seed. ACTUAL and NURSE share the template closure. NURSE's QUOTE and BODY→AUTHOR roles are compatible with the interpretation, but no occurrence-local RECALL replacement, source-object binding and acceptance consumer were recovered. This is a missing assembly step, not a calculation that can be repaired merely by substituting a same-spelled state from elsewhere. (B:The AIR/ACTUAL descendants and later admission failures; B:Tagged attribution and the Entry123 certificate)

Email 244 is a separate source gap: the described encrypted administrator-secret attachment was not recovered natively. A frozen prospective comparison would use its exact bytes and strict canonical OpenPGP binary form, with narrowly specified SHA256 halves and recipient identity. Rendered correspondence cannot reconstruct unavailable ciphertext, and the candidate is not declared a mandatory puzzle output. The 2015 “Not this again” email is another distinct case: preserved transport inconsistencies and contemporary requests for a signature make it useful evidence about the weakness of sender labels. It does not establish a causal 2015→2020→2021 protocol. The [preserved mail](https://gnusha.org/pi/bitcoindev/20151210065446.BU5Ev-eA85CGcmB3niSOBRJQ24ideF3F5VDZhCf4BCw%40z/) and [contemporary discussion](https://bitcointalk.org/index.php?topic=1283339.0) retain that context. (B:K24; B:K25; B:Historical PRIMARY: packet anatomy, missing payload and finite DSA tests)

## 11. The mixed Q packet and why its selection history matters

The later sweep's two Q signatures support a reproducible twelve-projection packet containing AUTHOR, ENVELOPE, SOURCE, PROOF, SCALE, VERIFY, FINAL, DISCOVER, WALL and ARTIST. The preserved formulas use native signature scalars/digests and publicly derived points. Some cells are eleven-bit values, two are byte endpoints, and one includes checksum material. Several depend on imported P2095. This is a heterogeneous projection recipe, not twelve uniform extractions from a single native packet. (B:The Q packet: exact reproduction and controlling provenance correction)

The twelve-order arrangement supplies a twenty-four-bit trailer, splitting into CHOOSE322, AIR43 and a comma. But swapping the two ENVELOPE-producing projections does not change the words, and an omitted ordinary V.x XOR cell would create a thirteen-entry, twenty-six-bit trailer. No independent boundary justifies that omission. The earliest retained selection history already contained desired features and order; later copies are descendants of that history, not independent discoveries. (B:The Q packet: exact reproduction and controlling provenance correction)

Within a fixed round-four census of 861 unordered signature pairs and 5,166 pair/operator cases, the frozen predicates select the final sweep pair. Each predicate already selects that same pair individually, so combining them does not add independent filtering. The reported 1/861 is descriptive within that family, not a discovery p-value. The AND interpretation remains separately unselected. Those qualifications preserve a selective exact pattern without using it to manufacture the entrance required by Email 260. (B:The Q packet: exact reproduction and controlling provenance correction)

Conditional AUTHOR[SUB] and ARTIST[SUB] closures each contain 3,626 definitions and share a selective STUMBLE[SUB] structure. Their immediate return sets meet at BUNKER. Across the full model, ENVELOPE occurs as a join terminal 3,061 times, with forty-five paths containing the exact GRAB token. Such structural counts are useful for finding and comparing model behavior. Same-spelled BUNKER, GRAB or PRIMARY occurrences do not inherit certificate, email or physical-frame provenance without a recorded transfer. A later check confirms Q.x's final byte is BRAIN215, but actual GRAB[SUB] outputs CARPET/PULSE/CATALOG, not PRIMARY. (B:Conditional Q-SUB closure and the frame seam; B:Parked infrastructure and settlement findings retain their narrower scope)

## 12. Source recovery produced authentic infrastructure, with narrower conclusions

Several lengthy investigations were worthwhile because they replaced vague historical claims with exact objects and explicit source limits. Their success should not be confused with an undiscovered continuation or attribution proof.

### 12.1 Genesis quotation and native commitments

The final eighteen bytes of exact362 are byte-for-byte equal to the middle component of genesis ExtraData: `We are all Satoshi`. The retained 3,110,907-byte genesis configuration, SHA256 `be1ea67ecc33f1effe3bac86c7b23f94916bdc654cbf7db0a80e3244e6038206`, supplies a 103-byte Data object and the native content needed to reproduce momentum hash `9e204601d1b7b1427fe12bc82622e610d8a6ad43c40abf020eb66e538bb8eeb0`. Its content includes the prize-account genesis header; the Burn references that header as previousHash. (B:Native proof representations and the genesis quotation reconstruction)

An audit-created quotation capsule packages these relationships. All 145 source signatures pass; altering the quote or a source byte rejects the source signature. Rotating intact address/signature pairs leaves native message verification unchanged but changes the publication-order hash. These controls explain exactly which properties are authenticated. They do not show that the signers endorsed Zenon genesis, that genesis copied specifically from this publication, or that the constructor intended the capsule as its serializer. (B:Native proof representations and the genesis quotation reconstruction)

A separate configuration test changes SporkAddress while preserving the same genesis hash, with both configurations passing native checks. Therefore that particular data commitment does not bind the configuration's upgrade authority. Ordinary account, momentum, history and enforcement receipts remain valid, but must not be promoted into a historical identity binding. (B:Parked infrastructure and settlement findings retain their narrower scope)

### 12.2 Rootstock and the Bitcoin anchor

The Rootstock investigation identified an exact uncle rather than the canonical block at the selected height. Uncle `0x2f896267d08cad258a82b8c81d48b97e1457264017c56458db6b5b4449511fc7`, height 3,870,562, verifies its native RLP hash, mining prefix, the exact Bitcoin 710968 header, a ten-hash coinbase branch and stated proof of work. Changed proof/prefix controls fail. Its time is 23 November 2021 at 11:00:17 UTC; the IRIS build text is ordinary client identity. (B:Parked infrastructure and settlement findings retain their narrower scope)

The selected contracts describe signed BTCUSD publication and aggregation, including five historical signers in a nine-member round and zero altered-message members. Some executable bytes match while metadata differs; one contract build remains unreproduced, and historical RPC slots/rounds lack independent state proofs. An earlier Bitcoin block already has an RSK tag from another pool. These findings establish real infrastructure. They do not select a new puzzle field or make unavailable sidechain bytes an active mandatory blocker. (B:Parked infrastructure and settlement findings retain their narrower scope)

### 12.3 Release packages and old software

The recovered February 2011 release announcement and initial Linux package contain real commitment information. The 9,833,353-byte Linux archive matches the original announced SHA1 `7dfbc05b36112f59886a29f044cfd21c6c253169`. A signed-tag ancestry capsule verifies twenty-two raw Git trees, three commits and two parent links. Of 113 package source members, 106 match committed bytes, four differ and three are absent. That authenticates matching source bytes relative to the signed anchor, not the entire unsigned archive or its binaries. (B:The historical release trail adds real commitments, not Q authority)

Only one of sixteen cross-comparisons between two four-file commitment lists matches both filename and full SHA1: the original Mac archive. Its bytes remain missing. Later recovered certification and release signatures implement an ordinary three-of-five software release policy. They provide an ordinary reason for the public key's packaging; they do not sign Email 260, Q or the artwork. (B:The historical release trail adds real commitments, not Q authority; B:A new certification and an ordinary software-verification role)

The Zenon v0.0.1 bundle investigation likewise distinguishes two release assets with an unexplained fourteen-byte difference. Ordinary repackaging is plausible, but no changed input was causally tied to that difference. The exact tag target/history, package assembly scripts, first controller tree, missing local SDK contents and some release-linked hashes prevent deterministic historical reconstruction. A later v0.0.3 checksum cannot authenticate v0.0.1. No qualifying historical-primary statement binding Q, the artwork or terminal was found in the native literal scans. (B:Version0.0.1 bundle recovery and the unexplained fourteen-byte change)

The historical received VERSION log supplies an exact little-endian version 100 and a source-conformant reconstruction of a 46-byte payload. Only twenty-five raw bytes are printed; the stock source supplies fifteen intervening candidate bytes. The endpoint 192.168.0.1:1531 is a receiver-side accepted-socket observation, not proof of the remote server's own address. An ephemeral port is plausible, while the private-address topology remains unresolved. A missing packet capture, modified build or runtime state cannot be recovered by interpreting local variable names as identity evidence. (B:K28)

## 13. Broad tests, rejected routes and the work that remains preserved

The baseline's negative results are valuable because they limit repeated searches and identify exactly what a future proposal must improve. The table below groups the principal families. A zero belongs to its declared population and predicate; it is never a worldwide absence claim. (B:K29; B:K30)

| Family | Retained result | Correct scope |
|---|---|---|
| Backward Keccak fit | One reported match among 69,120 state/stage candidates and 51,200 walk formulas | Known target, analyst-selected layout/stage/walk; rejected as a forward solve |
| ART/APES cultural equations | Two matched tweet controls give target rates 25.10% and 24.22%; uniform sampling gives 25.11% | Exact arithmetic is compatible with ordinary word availability; slogan completion remains interpretive |
| Literal q/1 carrier | Fixed direct/order/polarity and clue-XOR families yield no supported instruction | Six printable XOR outputs among 640 are near the simple null expectation; printability is not a message |
| Calendar interpretation | Seven of 256 constrained alternatives reproduce the selected calendar properties | Observed pair is nonunique under that geometry model |
| Signature DER lengths | Tested group/order/polarity readers fail | DER length aliases component high bits, not a separate free channel |
| Historical/message/Q joins | No same-role or P/K cross-role match in 44,425 pairs across 392 records | Rejects those equality predicates across Q42 + historical205 + message145 |
| Native authorizations | Zero matches in the registered RetrieveAssets and legacy-allocation/pillar comparisons | Large finite comparison populations do not exclude unrelated author-controlled keys |
| Eight final mechanisms | E001–E008 complete without a new instruction, point or historical claim | Prior exposure remains disclosed; no blind-prediction claim |
| Storage formats | Dedicated Berkeley DB/wallet.dat/addr.dat/blkindex.dat receipts not fully recovered | A named coverage gap, not an invented exhaustive negative |

The eight final mechanisms cover first/last-letter selection by q/1; parity or one of eleven source bits; unsalted SHA256 watermarking of fixed native inputs; an exact Q-derived empty-tree Taproot address; every affine Boolean and integer-threshold rule within four fixed systems; punctuation/q/1 prefix selection; Hamming(15,11) packets; and the complete mixed 156-bit stream. E002 gives zero exact matches among ninety-six candidates; E003 zero among eight; E006 zero meaningful complete readings among sixteen; E007 zero complete valid packets among sixty-four. The results and full unhelpful outputs are preserved. (B:Eight final mechanism tests add bounded negatives)

E005 is particularly strong within its scope: all 4,096 affine rules and 4,098 threshold rules were checked per fixed system. Each affine system has rank 12 and augmented rank 13, proving inconsistency. A four-cell source XOR relation gives zero while the corresponding glyph bits XOR to one; no affine reader can satisfy both. This rules out that entire fixed family, not arbitrary nonlinear codes or a rendering process. (B:Eight final mechanism tests add bounded negatives)

E004's exact standard Q-derived Taproot address has empty confirmed and mempool history in two indexes at the recorded 11 October 2026 snapshot. Seven official BIP341 vectors and independent public-tweak checks pass. The existing Q-pubkeyhash history remains twenty-five confirmed transactions, forty-two funded and forty-two spent outputs, with the latest known sweep on 26 January 2022. Two additional records are incoming preconstruction funding, not later Q messages. Agreement between indexes is useful history evidence, not cryptographic proof of universal absence. (B:Eight final mechanism tests add bounded negatives)

The steganography audit also fixed several capacity mistakes. Artwork rows contain printable ASCII and ordinary spaces, without hidden Unicode, tabs or alternate-space identities. The Base64 encodings are canonical; all ten unused padding bits are zero. After replacing row artwork and parent-output index, the nineteen stripped row transactions reduce to one template. The 192 z glyphs provide no identity variation at fixed sites. Deterministic hashes, overlapping encodings and repeated graph order cannot simply be added together as independent hidden-message capacity. (B:What the steganography book audit actually adds)

One art/witness correspondence survives uncommon but post-discovery controls. Among 149,652,360 pair-preserving assignments, 30,808 pass its full retained predicate, about one in 4,858. A later five-field test uses one million whole-pair permutations: native nonce-y LSB passes 224 times, published-s LSB 355 times, and the other three fields zero times; the any-field family has 579 hits. The observed native nonce-y specificity is worth retaining. It still needs an independently selected message, checksum or verifier before becoming evidence of an extracted payload. (B:What the steganography book audit actually adds)

Database labels require the same discipline. A wallet.dat claim needs a recognizable Berkeley DB structure and coherent parser result. A word stream called DATABASE does not acquire a file type through its name. The baseline explicitly had not recovered every dedicated requested storage test. Missing legacy database clones also limit historical xStake reconstruction. This master preserves that gap instead of converting an asserted earlier negative into a numerical result with no receipt. (B:K27)

### 13.1 The methodological corrections are findings

Several corrections recur because they prevented different branches from being spliced together without evidence: old punctuation/2905 packing was superseded; P00 was not proved executable; a fingerprint was not a generic CALL instruction; ABANDON did not automatically return to a parent; a search's depth-first order did not create a return barrier; residual bits did not automatically become new words; COPY did not become an opcode through its dictionary label; a seven-bit match did not restore an APPLE occurrence; and Taproot-specific hidden fields cannot exist inside these SegWit-v0 spends merely because the puzzle is named Taproot. (B:K30)

These are corrections to edges and claims, not erasures of the observations. Genuine historical signatures remain genuine when attribution is withdrawn. A source file remains informative when its puzzle selector fails. Exact model outputs remain reproducible under their stated compiler when its initial admission is open. The complete source/evidence preservation is what allows those distinctions to survive revision. (B:K30; B:Coverage and preservation contract)

### 13.2 The honest October 13 position

At the historical cutoff, the primary treasure is solved and independently checkable. The public transaction construction, GCM completion, mnemonic derivation, native prize account and retained account actions are concrete results. The artifact also contains an extensive set of reproducible source-local and conditional relationships. The unresolved step is an independently established continuation that supplies its own input, operation, source and verifier without importing the answer from a later object. (B:Authenticated primary solve; B:Cross-object negatives and the controlling current boundary)

Possible future evidence remains varied: an independently dated production input or recipe; an issuer-controlled disclosure with its context; an original decoder or commitment selecting an exact next object; a qualified native email source; or a historical secret-dependent signature over an intelligible exact construction claim. None of those is declared the one file that must exist, and completed exposed-decoder or source searches do not become new evidence simply by being repeated. Later chapters record which of these boundaries changed after October 13. (B:Remaining gaps after these campaigns; B:Current proof gaps and supersession rules)


# Part II — The October continuation

The middle of October changed the investigation from a collection of promising word readings into a reproducible sequence of proposed image operations. A two-row panel could be installed in a separate working representation of the artwork; an eleven-cell object called GLASS could be moved; a checked PULL TWENTY instruction appeared before its own one-bit repair; and the resulting reader selected a genuine Bitcoin signature. Independent checks confirmed important native transactions, signature fields and source-byte relationships. ([R0089](#source-r0089); [R0230](#source-r0230); [R0340](#source-r0340); [R0352](#source-r0352))

The central qualification also became clearer. Reproducing a proposed interpreter does not establish that its author supplied every rule. A valid Bitcoin signature authenticates the original transaction, while a four-bit checksum provides a much smaller consistency check on a proposed reading. Neither automatically authorizes the next operation. Several October experiments succeeded as computations and then stopped at an unresolved source selector, data type, instruction pointer or return rule. This chapter preserves those stops alongside the successes. The results below are the recorded outcomes of the cited reports, not a claim that all experiments were rerun for this edition. ([R0344](#source-r0344); [R0482](#source-r0482); [R0489](#source-r0489))
